[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F03, E03.9, G30.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, welcome in—I'm Dr. [Name]. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello, Dr. [Name]. I suppose... well, my daughter is the one who really insisted I come see you, but I do see her point, I really do. It's just that lately, my head feels so terribly foggy. It's like trying to think through a thick bowl of pea soup, if you know what I mean. I've always been sharp—I mean, I've managed the local historical society's volunteer schedule for over fifteen years, and that's no small feat, let me tell you. But recently, I just keep mixing up the simplest things. Just last week, I completely jumbled the dates for our new quilt exhibit. I had people showing up on a Tuesday when the opening wasn't until Thursday, and oh, it was just a mess. And the phone calls! I'll pick up the receiver, dial a number, and halfway through it ringing, I can't for the life of me remember if I've already called Mary or if I was supposed to be calling Susan about the bake sale. It's so frustrating because I know the information is in there somewhere, but I just can't seem to grasp it. It’s making me feel quite foolish, honestly, and it's just not like me to be so... scattered. I mean, I even showed up an hour early for this appointment today because I was so worried I’d get the time wrong again!\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me when you first started noticing this fogginess and memory trouble, and whether it has been getting worse gradually over time or came on more suddenly?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... it hasn't been forever, certainly not. I'd say it's been about four months now. Yes, four months. It all started right around the time we had that dreadful water damage in the house. Oh, you wouldn't believe the mess! A pipe burst right above the downstairs half-bath, and water just went everywhere. It soaked into the drywall, ruined my favorite runner in the hallway—the one I got at that estate sale down in Springfield, such a shame—and it forced us into this whole remodeling project that we hadn't planned on at all. \n\nAnd you know how contractors are, they say two weeks and it turns into two months, and there's dust everywhere, and strangers walking through your home at all hours. It was just so disruptive. Anyway, that's right when I started noticing the fogginess. At first, I thought it was just the stress of the remodel, you know? Trying to keep track of paint swatches and tile samples and when the plumber was supposed to come back. But the remodel is mostly done now, thank goodness, except for a bit of trim they still need to paint, and my head still feels just as thick. So I suppose it didn't come on all at once like a light switch, but it definitely started during that awful water damage mess about four months ago, and it just hasn't lifted since. It's just hung around like a bad cold that you can't shake.\n\nDoctor: Since this fogginess started, have you noticed changes in your mood—like feeling unusually sad, anxious, or worried most of the time?\n\nPatient: Well, goodness... sad or anxious? I don't... I don't really think so. I mean, certainly, the whole ordeal with the house was stressful, as I was saying. Anyone would be worried when there are fans blowing day and night to dry out the floorboards, and you're just praying mold doesn't set in because my husband, God rest his soul, always used to say mold is the silent killer of a good foundation. But as for just sitting around feeling sad or... or unusually worried now that the worst of it is over? No, I wouldn't say that. \n\nI'm frustrated, certainly! Frustrated that I can't remember if I put the kettle on, or why I walked into the kitchen in the first place. Just yesterday, I went into the pantry for a tin of crushed tomatoes for my meatloaf—I always use the crushed ones, not the diced, because the diced just never break down right in the sauce—and I stood there staring at the cans for what felt like ten minutes, completely blank. But it's not sadness, dear. It's just... it's just this awful feeling of being disorganized in my own mind. I'm not crying into my tea or anything of that sort, and I'm not pacing the floors with worry, except for worrying about why my brain feels like it's stuffed with cotton wool. So, no, my mood itself is fine, it's just my memory that's driving me up the wall!\n\nDoctor: Over these past four months, would you say your memory and thinking have stayed about the same, or have they been gradually getting worse and worse?\n\nPatient: Well, that's a very good question, Doctor. I've been trying to put my finger on that very thing myself. I suppose... well, I wouldn't say it's a straight line down, if you know what I mean. It's not like every day is noticeably worse than the day before, like watching a tire slowly go flat. No, it's more like... it's just stayed consistently foggy since it started. \n\nI mean, I'm not entirely sure, to be perfectly honest. Some days I think, \"Oh, I'm doing a bit better today,\" like last Sunday when I managed to get through the entire church service and remember the pastor's main points for discussion at coffee hour afterward—we were talking about the book of Ruth, such a lovely story of loyalty, don't you think? But then the very next day, Monday, I'll go to the grocery store with a list of three things—just milk, bread, and those little peppermint candies I like to keep in my purse—and I'll come home with the milk and the peppermints, but completely forget the bread! And I'll have walked right past the bakery section, too. \n\nSo, I don't think it's necessarily getting worse and worse, like some sort of terrible decline. It just feels like I've hit this... this plateau of forgetfulness, and I'm just stuck here. It's been about the same level of frustrating for the whole four months. Just consistently thick and muddled, without really dipping lower, but certainly not clearing up either. It's just terribly stubborn, this fogginess.\n\nDoctor: Around the time your symptoms began during the home remodel, did you have any illnesses, infections, head injuries, or exposure to fumes, chemicals, or mold that you’re aware of?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that... illnesses or injuries. No, no head injuries, thank goodness. I've always been very careful on the stairs, especially after my friend Martha took a tumble last winter and fractured her hip—she was in rehab for weeks, poor thing, and the food there was just atrocious, she said. So I always hold the handrail. And I haven't been sick, not even a sniffle, which is a blessing because usually when the seasons change, I get a bit of a tickle in my throat.\n\nNow, as for the house... well, like I mentioned before, my husband always warned about mold. \"The silent killer,\" he called it. So when the pipe burst, that was my absolute biggest fear. I had them bring in those giant industrial fans—oh, the noise they made, it was like living on an airport runway! I had to turn the television up so loud just to hear Jeopardy. But the remediation company—such nice young men, very polite, always took their boots off at the door—they assured me they got everything dried out before any mold could start growing. They used these little moisture meter gadgets on the walls and everything. \n\nAnd chemicals or fumes? Well, there was the smell of the new paint, of course, when they finally got around to fixing the drywall. I chose a lovely shade called \"Alabaster\" for the hallway, it really brightens up the space compared to that dreary beige we had before. But it was just regular latex paint, nothing terribly strong, and I made sure to keep the windows cracked, even though it was a bit chilly, just to keep the air moving. I don't think I breathed in anything I shouldn't have. So, really, besides the dust from the drywall and the stress of having my entire downstairs turned upside down, I can't think of any sickness or strange fumes or anything like that. Just the ordinary chaos of a remodel, but nothing out of the ordinary, if you see what I mean.\n\nDoctor: Has this memory fogginess caused any real problems with managing your daily tasks—like paying bills, taking medications correctly, or handling your usual responsibilities—or have you still been able to keep up with everything despite the frustration?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the rub, isn't it? That's what really finally made me realize I couldn't just brush this off as being tired from the house repairs. Because, yes, it actually has started causing a mess of things, and I've always prided myself on being organized. I mean, I used to keep the whole household running like a Swiss watch! But lately... oh, goodness, it's just been one thing after another. \n\nFor instance, I completely missed my appointment with Dr. Henderson—my podiatrist, you know, he’s been helping me with this dreadful plantar fasciitis, which is a whole other story, let me tell you, the pain in the mornings! But I just completely forgot to go. Didn't even write it on the kitchen calendar. And then there’s the senior center. I volunteer there on Tuesdays and Thursdays, helping coordinate the activities schedule—bingo, the knitting circle, the guest speakers. Well, I got the dates so tangled up a few weeks ago that we had the watercolor instructor showing up at the exact same time as the gentleman who does the estate planning seminars! It was terribly embarrassing. We had to squeeze the painters into the small kitchenette area, and nobody was happy. \n\nIt’s gotten to the point where... well, it’s hard to admit, but my husband—bless his heart, he tries, even though he's usually the one asking me where his glasses are—he’s had to step in. He's been the one making the phone calls to the contractors to finish up the baseboards, and he's taken over managing the calendar because I just can't seem to keep it all straight anymore. I used to handle all of those decisions myself, easily! I’d juggle the bills, the appointments, the repairmen... and now I'm just looking at the paperwork and it feels like reading Greek. So, yes, it's definitely spilling over into my daily life, and I absolutely hate feeling like I have to be supervised in my own home.\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\": \"Screen for a reversible medication-induced cause (e.g., new sleep aids, anticholinergics, or other OTC/prescription drugs started during the stress\n\nPatient: Well, medications... let me think about that. You know, it's funny you ask, because my daughter—she's a nurse, actually, over at County General, works in the pediatric wing, such a demanding job but she loves the little ones—she's always asking me, \"Mom, what are you taking? Are you sure you're not mixing things up?\" And I tell her, I say, \"Susan, I am very careful with my pills.\" \n\nI have one of those little plastic organizers, you know the ones? With the days of the week printed on them in big bold letters. Mine has different colors for morning and evening—blue for the morning, because it reminds me of the sky, and a sort of dusky purple for the evening. It really helps keep things straight, especially now that things are so foggy. \n\nBut as for *new* medications... no, I really don't think so. I mean, I take my usual things. There's the little white pill for my blood pressure that Dr. Miller put me on years ago—oh, gosh, it must be five or six years now, ever since that time I got so worked up over the neighbor's dog digging up my prize-winning petunias. And then there's my calcium supplement, because at my age, you really have to watch your bone density, like I said about my friend Martha's hip. I take a multivitamin too, the kind for women over fifty, though frankly I've been over fifty for quite a while now! \n\nDuring the worst of the remodel stress, when the noise was just unbearable and the dust was everywhere, I did have a few nights where I just couldn't drift off. I'd lie there staring at the ceiling, worrying about whether the insurance was going to cover the new flooring in the hallway. I might have taken a Tylenol PM once or twice, just to help me settle down. But that was months ago, and just a couple of times. I certainly haven't started taking anything new on a regular basis, no new prescriptions or anything like that. Just my same old routine, the same little blue and purple boxes every day.\n\nDoctor: Since the remodel began, how has your sleep been—are you sleeping through the night restfully, or do you notice snoring, gasping, or feeling unrefreshed when you wake up?\n\nPatient: Well, as for snoring or gasping, my husband hasn't mentioned a peep about anything like that, and he is a terribly light sleeper. If a car door slams three houses down the street, he's sitting bolt upright in bed! So I think my breathing is just fine in the night. And I *am* sleeping, I really am. I close my eyes and sleep right through till morning. \n\nBut restfully? Oh, doctor, that’s exactly the problem, and it's been driving me absolutely batty. I don't know why, but I wake up just so terribly tired even after sleeping a full eight or nine hours. It makes no sense! I’ll pull myself out of bed—and we have a lovely, heavy down comforter, so it's always a little hard to leave it anyway, especially when the mornings are crisp—and I’ll go into the bathroom to wash up. I just do my normal routine, you know, wash my face with a little warm water, put on my face cream, brush my teeth. Then I head downstairs to the kitchen to make breakfast. \n\nAnd it’s nothing complicated I'm making! Just putting the kettle on the stove for a cup of Earl Grey tea and popping a piece of wheat bread in the toaster. Maybe slicing half a grapefruit if they looked decent at the grocery store. But I tell you, by the time I wash up and make that simple little breakfast, I feel like I need to sit down again. I'm just completely wiped out. I'll get my plate to the kitchen table and just slump into my chair, and I haven't even really done anything yet! It’s so aggravating, feeling this completely drained just from getting the day started. I used to be up and at 'em, ready to tackle the laundry or the gardening, and now just buttering my toast feels like a chore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes like feeling unusually cold, dry skin, constipation, or weight gain over these past few months?\n\nPatient: Weight gain? Oh, goodness, no, it’s actually been quite the opposite! As for being cold or the dry skin, well, nothing out of the ordinary for a woman my age, I suppose. I used to love cooking a nice, hearty pot roast on Sundays, with the tender carrots and the little red potatoes soaking up all the juices, but now... well, the thought of a big meal just makes me want to turn my head away. I look in the refrigerator and absolutely nothing looks appetizing. \n\nAnd because I'm just pecking like a little bird all day, my clothes are absolutely swimming on me! I went to put on my favorite pleated skirt last Sunday for church—the navy blue one I got at Macy's a few years ago, such lovely quality, you really don't find stitching like that anymore—and it has gotten so terribly loose! It practically slid right down my hips. I had to use a safety pin just to keep it up around my waist. I certainly haven't been trying to lose weight, heaven knows I don't need to be going on any diets at seventy-nine, you don't want to get too frail at this age. But without me even trying, the pounds are just dropping off and my skirts are hanging off me just because I can't bring myself to eat a proper meal.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like gardening, church activities, or spending time with friends, even if you don't feel sad?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s... it’s strange you say that, because that’s exactly it. I have always taken such pride in my tomatoes. Every spring, I’d get the soil just right, mix in a little peat moss and fertilizer—my husband used to help me with the heavy bags before his knees got so bad—and I’d plant these beautiful, sturdy little vines. I just loved the smell of the leaves when you brush up against them, you know that smell? But lately? The pots are just sitting there on the back patio. I look out the window and I know they need tending, I know the weeds are probably choking them out, but I just... I can't make myself care. \n\nAnd it’s the same with watching the birds. Right past the patio, we have this lovely cedar bird feeder my son-in-law built for me. I used to sit by the window every single morning, just waiting for the cardinals out back. There was this one bright red male, oh, he was just gorgeous, always so bossy with the other birds. I used to get such a thrill seeing him. Now, I see a flash of red out of the corner of my eye and I don't even bother to turn my head to look. \n\nEven my watercolors! I have a whole little setup in the corner of the sunroom. I took a class at the community center oh, it must be fifteen years ago now, and I just fell in love with painting little landscapes and flowers. I have all my good sable brushes laid out, my nice thick pressed paper, the little tubes of paint... and they're just gathering dust. I walk right past them. \n\nAnd the line dancing, too. My friends and I used to go down to the senior center every Thursday afternoon for line dancing. We’d do the Electric Slide, the Boot Scootin' Boogie, all of those fun ones. It was such a hoot, and such wonderful exercise for the joints. But when Shirley called me last week to see if I was coming, I just made up an excuse. I told her I was busy organizing the pantry. But I wasn't busy. I just couldn't muster up a single ounce of interest to go. It’s like all these wonderful things I used to love are just sitting there, waiting for me to pick them back up, and I just look at them and feel... nothing. I just can't make myself care about them anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. {"candidates": ["F32.9", "F03", "G30.9"], "note": "Removed E03.9 (hypothyroidism) because significant unintentional weight loss and lack of cold intolerance/constipation argue against it. The prominent anhedonia (loss of interest in gardening, birdwatching, painting, dancing) combined with poor appetite/weight loss, fatigue, and non-progressive 'plateaued' cognitive fog is highly suggestive of a depressive syndrome with pseudodementia features, strengthening F32.9. F03 and G30.9 remain as differentials for an organic dementia process given persistent memory/executive complaints, though the stable (non-progressive) course over 4 months argues somewhat against classic neurodegenerative decline.", "is_final": false}

I certainly haven't been trying to lose weight, heaven knows I don't need to be going on any diets at seventy-nine, you don't want to get too frail at this age. But without me even trying, the pounds are just dropping off and my skirts are hanging off me just because I can't bring myself to eat a proper meal.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like gardening, church activities, or spending time with friends, even if you don't feel sad?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s... it’s strange you say that, because that’s exactly it. I’m not sitting around crying into my handkerchief all day, I really aren't, but... well, the things I used to just love, they just sit there now. \n\nTake my tomato plants, for instance. I have always taken such pride in my tomatoes. Every spring, I’d get the soil just right, mix in a little peat moss and fertilizer—my husband used to help me with the heavy bags before his knees got so bad—and I’d plant these beautiful, sturdy little vines. I just loved the smell of the leaves when you brush up against them, you know that smell? But lately? The pots are just sitting there on the back patio. I look out the window and I know they need tending, I know the weeds are probably choking them out, but I just... I can't make myself care. \n\nAnd it’s the same with watching the birds. Right past the patio, we have this lovely cedar bird feeder my son-in-law built for me. I used to sit by the window every single morning, just waiting for the cardinals out back. There was this one bright red male, oh, he was just gorgeous, always so bossy with the other birds. I used to get such a thrill seeing him. Now, I see a flash of red out of the corner of my eye and I don't even bother to turn my head to look. \n\nEven my watercolors! I have a whole little setup in the corner of the sunroom. I took a class at the community center oh, it must be fifteen years ago now, and I just fell in love with painting little landscapes and flowers. I have all my good sable brushes laid out, my nice thick pressed paper, the little tubes of paint... and they're just gathering dust. I walk right past them. \n\nAnd the line dancing, too. My friends and I used to go down to the senior center every Thursday afternoon for line dancing. We’d do the Electric Slide, the Boot Scootin' Boogie, all of those fun ones. It was such a hoot, and such wonderful exercise for the joints. But when Shirley called me last week to see if I was coming, I just made up an excuse. I told her I was busy organizing the pantry. But I wasn't busy. I just couldn't muster up a single ounce of interest to go. It’s like all these wonderful things I used to love are just sitting there, waiting for me to pick them back up, and I just look at them and feel... nothing. Oh, heavens, no! No, no, no. I might be terribly tired, and my tomatoes might be dying on the vine, but I am certainly not ready to give up the ghost just yet! 

Life is absolutely worth living. I mean, I have seven grandchildren! The youngest, little Timmy, he just started middle school this fall, and he plays the trombone in the school band. Not very well yet, mind you, it sounds a bit like a dying goose sometimes when he practices, but I want to be around to hear him get better at it. I want to see him graduate. So no thoughts like that at all, goodness me. 

And as for being a burden or feeling worthless... well, I just don't see it that way. My husband, bless his heart, he tells me all the time he doesn't know what he'd do without me. Even if I'm just slumped in my armchair these days while he watches his golf tournaments on the television—you know how the announcers always whisper on those golf broadcasts? It used to drive me absolutely crazy, but now it's just sort of soothing background noise. Anyway, he just likes knowing I'm in the room with him. We've been married fifty-six years this coming August, and we take care of each other. I'm certainly not worthless to him. 

And my daughter Susan, she lives just two towns over, and she calls me every single Sunday after church without fail. She always says, 'Mom, you and Dad are the glue holding this family together.' So I certainly don't feel like a burden to them. If anything, they're always asking if they can come over and help *me* with things! Just last week Susan offered to come over and clean out the gutters, though I told her absolutely not, she has her own house and her own yard to worry about, and Arthur can hire the neighbor boy to do it if it really needs doing. 

So no, doctor, I don't have any guilt or awful thoughts about not wanting to be here. I don't feel like a burden in the slightest. I just... I just don't have the pep I used to have, and I can't find the joy in my little hobbies right now. But I know my place in the world, and I know my family loves me. I just wish I had the energy to be my normal self for them.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello, Dr. [Name]. I suppose... well, my daughter is the one who really insisted I come see you, but I do see her point, I really do. It's just that lately, my head feels so terribly foggy. It's like trying to think through a thick bowl of pea soup, if you know what I mean. I've always been sharp—I mean, I've managed the local historical society's volunteer schedule for over fifteen years, and that's no small feat, let me tell you. But recently, I just keep mixing up the simplest things. Just last week, I completely jumbled the dates for our new quilt exhibit. I had people showing up on a Tuesday when the opening wasn't until Thursday, and oh, it was just a mess. And the phone calls! I'll pick up the receiver, dial a number, and halfway through it ringing, I can't for the life of me remember if I've already called Mary or if I was supposed to be calling Susan about the bake sale. It's so frustrating because I know the information is in there somewhere, but I just can't seem to grasp it. It’s making me feel quite foolish, honestly, and it's just not like me to be so... scattered. I mean, I even showed up an hour early for this appointment today because I was so worried I’d get the time wrong again!\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me when you first started noticing this fogginess and memory trouble, and whether it has been getting worse gradually over time or came on more suddenly?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... it hasn't been forever, certainly not. I'd say it's been about four months now. Yes, four months. It all started right around the time we had that dreadful water damage in the house. Oh, you wouldn't believe the mess! A pipe burst right above the downstairs half-bath, and water just went everywhere. It soaked into the drywall, ruined my favorite runner in the hallway—the one I got at that estate sale down in Springfield, such a shame—and it forced us into this whole remodeling project that we hadn't planned on at all. \n\nAnd you know how contractors are, they say two weeks and it turns into two months, and there's dust everywhere, and strangers walking through your home at all hours. It was just so disruptive. Anyway, that's right when I started noticing the fogginess. At first, I thought it was just the stress of the remodel, you know? Trying to keep track of paint swatches and tile samples and when the plumber was supposed to come back. But the remodel is mostly done now, thank goodness, except for a bit of trim they still need to paint, and my head still feels just as thick. So I suppose it didn't come on all at once like a light switch, but it definitely started during that awful water damage mess about four months ago, and it just hasn't lifted since. It's just hung around like a bad cold that you can't shake.\n\nDoctor: Since this fogginess started, have you noticed changes in your mood—like feeling unusually sad, anxious, or worried most of the time?\n\nPatient: Well, goodness... sad or anxious? I don't... I don't really think so. I mean, certainly, the whole ordeal with the house was stressful, as I was saying. Anyone would be worried when there are fans blowing day and night to dry out the floorboards, and you're just praying mold doesn't set in because my husband, God rest his soul, always used to say mold is the silent killer of a good foundation. But as for just sitting around feeling sad or... or unusually worried now that the worst of it is over? No, I wouldn't say that. \n\nI'm frustrated, certainly! Frustrated that I can't remember if I put the kettle on, or why I walked into the kitchen in the first place. Just yesterday, I went into the pantry for a tin of crushed tomatoes for my meatloaf—I always use the crushed ones, not the diced, because the diced just never break down right in the sauce—and I stood there staring at the cans for what felt like ten minutes, completely blank. But it's not sadness, dear. It's just... it's just this awful feeling of being disorganized in my own mind. I'm not crying into my tea or anything of that sort, and I'm not pacing the floors with worry, except for worrying about why my brain feels like it's stuffed with cotton wool. So, no, my mood itself is fine, it's just my memory that's driving me up the wall!\n\nDoctor: Over these past four months, would you say your memory and thinking have stayed about the same, or have they been gradually getting worse and worse?\n\nPatient: Well, that's a very good question, Doctor. I've been trying to put my finger on that very thing myself. I suppose... well, I wouldn't say it's a straight line down, if you know what I mean. It's not like every day is noticeably worse than the day before, like watching a tire slowly go flat. No, it's more like... it's just stayed consistently foggy since it started. \n\nI mean, I'm not entirely sure, to be perfectly honest. Some days I think, \"Oh, I'm doing a bit better today,\" like last Sunday when I managed to get through the entire church service and remember the pastor's main points for discussion at coffee hour afterward—we were talking about the book of Ruth, such a lovely story of loyalty, don't you think? But then the very next day, Monday, I'll go to the grocery store with a list of three things—just milk, bread, and those little peppermint candies I like to keep in my purse—and I'll come home with the milk and the peppermints, but completely forget the bread! And I'll have walked right past the bakery section, too. \n\nSo, I don't think it's necessarily getting worse and worse, like some sort of terrible decline. It just feels like I've hit this... this plateau of forgetfulness, and I'm just stuck here. It's been about the same level of frustrating for the whole four months. Just consistently thick and muddled, without really dipping lower, but certainly not clearing up either. It's just terribly stubborn, this fogginess.\n\nDoctor: Around the time your symptoms began during the home remodel, did you have any illnesses, infections, head injuries, or exposure to fumes, chemicals, or mold that you’re aware of?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that... illnesses or injuries. No, no head injuries, thank goodness. I've always been very careful on the stairs, especially after my friend Martha took a tumble last winter and fractured her hip—she was in rehab for weeks, poor thing, and the food there was just atrocious, she said. So I always hold the handrail. And I haven't been sick, not even a sniffle, which is a blessing because usually when the seasons change, I get a bit of a tickle in my throat.\n\nNow, as for the house... well, like I mentioned before, my husband always warned about mold. \"The silent killer,\" he called it. So when the pipe burst, that was my absolute biggest fear. I had them bring in those giant industrial fans—oh, the noise they made, it was like living on an airport runway! I had to turn the television up so loud just to hear Jeopardy. But the remediation company—such nice young men, very polite, always took their boots off at the door—they assured me they got everything dried out before any mold could start growing. They used these little moisture meter gadgets on the walls and everything. \n\nAnd chemicals or fumes? Well, there was the smell of the new paint, of course, when they finally got around to fixing the drywall. I chose a lovely shade called \"Alabaster\" for the hallway, it really brightens up the space compared to that dreary beige we had before. But it was just regular latex paint, nothing terribly strong, and I made sure to keep the windows cracked, even though it was a bit chilly, just to keep the air moving. I don't think I breathed in anything I shouldn't have. So, really, besides the dust from the drywall and the stress of having my entire downstairs turned upside down, I can't think of any sickness or strange fumes or anything like that. Just the ordinary chaos of a remodel, but nothing out of the ordinary, if you see what I mean.\n\nDoctor: Has this memory fogginess caused any real problems with managing your daily tasks—like paying bills, taking medications correctly, or handling your usual responsibilities—or have you still been able to keep up with everything despite the frustration?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the rub, isn't it? That's what really finally made me realize I couldn't just brush this off as being tired from the house repairs. Because, yes, it actually has started causing a mess of things, and I've always prided myself on being organized. I mean, I used to keep the whole household running like a Swiss watch! But lately... oh, goodness, it's just been one thing after another. \n\nFor instance, I completely missed my appointment with Dr. Henderson—my podiatrist, you know, he’s been helping me with this dreadful plantar fasciitis, which is a whole other story, let me tell you, the pain in the mornings! But I just completely forgot to go. Didn't even write it on the kitchen calendar. And then there’s the senior center. I volunteer there on Tuesdays and Thursdays, helping coordinate the activities schedule—bingo, the knitting circle, the guest speakers. Well, I got the dates so tangled up a few weeks ago that we had the watercolor instructor showing up at the exact same time as the gentleman who does the estate planning seminars! It was terribly embarrassing. We had to squeeze the painters into the small kitchenette area, and nobody was happy. \n\nIt’s gotten to the point where... well, it’s hard to admit, but my husband—bless his heart, he tries, even though he's usually the one asking me where his glasses are—he’s had to step in. He's been the one making the phone calls to the contractors to finish up the baseboards, and he's taken over managing the calendar because I just can't seem to keep it all straight anymore. I used to handle all of those decisions myself, easily! I’d juggle the bills, the appointments, the repairmen... and now I'm just looking at the paperwork and it feels like reading Greek. So, yes, it's definitely spilling over into my daily life, and I absolutely hate feeling like I have to be supervised in my own home.\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\": \"Screen for a reversible medication-induced cause (e.g., new sleep aids, anticholinergics, or other OTC/prescription drugs started during the stress\n\nPatient: Well, medications... let me think about that. You know, it's funny you ask, because my daughter—she's a nurse, actually, over at County General, works in the pediatric wing, such a demanding job but she loves the little ones—she's always asking me, \"Mom, what are you taking? Are you sure you're not mixing things up?\" And I tell her, I say, \"Susan, I am very careful with my pills.\" \n\nI have one of those little plastic organizers, you know the ones? With the days of the week printed on them in big bold letters. Mine has different colors for morning and evening—blue for the morning, because it reminds me of the sky, and a sort of dusky purple for the evening. It really helps keep things straight, especially now that things are so foggy. \n\nBut as for *new* medications... no, I really don't think so. I mean, I take my usual things. There's the little white pill for my blood pressure that Dr. Miller put me on years ago—oh, gosh, it must be five or six years now, ever since that time I got so worked up over the neighbor's dog digging up my prize-winning petunias. And then there's my calcium supplement, because at my age, you really have to watch your bone density, like I said about my friend Martha's hip. I take a multivitamin too, the kind for women over fifty, though frankly I've been over fifty for quite a while now! \n\nDuring the worst of the remodel stress, when the noise was just unbearable and the dust was everywhere, I did have a few nights where I just couldn't drift off. I'd lie there staring at the ceiling, worrying about whether the insurance was going to cover the new flooring in the hallway. I might have taken a Tylenol PM once or twice, just to help me settle down. But that was months ago, and just a couple of times. I certainly haven't started taking anything new on a regular basis, no new prescriptions or anything like that. Just my same old routine, the same little blue and purple boxes every day.\n\nDoctor: Since the remodel began, how has your sleep been—are you sleeping through the night restfully, or do you notice snoring, gasping, or feeling unrefreshed when you wake up?\n\nPatient: Well, as for snoring or gasping, my husband hasn't mentioned a peep about anything like that, and he is a terribly light sleeper. If a car door slams three houses down the street, he's sitting bolt upright in bed! So I think my breathing is just fine in the night. And I *am* sleeping, I really am. I close my eyes and sleep right through till morning. \n\nBut restfully? Oh, doctor, that’s exactly the problem, and it's been driving me absolutely batty. I don't know why, but I wake up just so terribly tired even after sleeping a full eight or nine hours. It makes no sense! I’ll pull myself out of bed—and we have a lovely, heavy down comforter, so it's always a little hard to leave it anyway, especially when the mornings are crisp—and I’ll go into the bathroom to wash up. I just do my normal routine, you know, wash my face with a little warm water, put on my face cream, brush my teeth. Then I head downstairs to the kitchen to make breakfast. \n\nAnd it’s nothing complicated I'm making! Just putting the kettle on the stove for a cup of Earl Grey tea and popping a piece of wheat bread in the toaster. Maybe slicing half a grapefruit if they looked decent at the grocery store. But I tell you, by the time I wash up and make that simple little breakfast, I feel like I need to sit down again. I'm just completely wiped out. I'll get my plate to the kitchen table and just slump into my chair, and I haven't even really done anything yet! It’s so aggravating, feeling this completely drained just from getting the day started. I used to be up and at 'em, ready to tackle the laundry or the gardening, and now just buttering my toast feels like a chore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes like feeling unusually cold, dry skin, constipation, or weight gain over these past few months?\n\nPatient: Weight gain? Oh, goodness, no, it’s actually been quite the opposite! As for being cold or the dry skin, well, nothing out of the ordinary for a woman my age, I suppose. But the weight, now that is something I've definitely noticed, and it ties right back into what I was just saying about making breakfast. \n\nYou see, when I make that toast, I hardly even eat it. Most days I just sort of... pick at it. I'll pick at a piece of toast in the morning, or maybe have a few spoonfuls of soup for lunch, like a light tomato bisque or just some plain chicken broth. Food just simply doesn't sound good to me lately. It’s the strangest thing. I used to love cooking a nice, hearty pot roast on Sundays, with the tender carrots and the little red potatoes soaking up all the juices, but now... well, the thought of a big meal just makes me want to turn my head away. I look out the window and I know they need tending, I know the weeds are probably choking them out, but I just... I can't make myself care. \n\nAnd it’s the same with watching the birds. But I wasn't busy. I just couldn't muster up a single ounce of interest to go. It’s like all these wonderful things I used to love are just sitting there, waiting for me to pick them back up, and I just look at them and feel... nothing. Not very well yet, mind you, it sounds a bit like a dying goose sometimes when he practices, but I want to be around to hear him get better at it. I want to see him graduate. I don't feel like a burden in the slightest. I just... I just don't have the pep I used to have, and I can't find the joy in my little hobbies right now. {"candidates": ["F32.9", "F03"], "note": "The prominent anhedonia across multiple domains (gardening, painting, birdwatching, dancing), significant unintentional weight loss/poor appetite, and non-restorative fatigue, combined with the non-progressive, stable 'plateau' course of cognitive fog tied temporally to a stressful life event, strongly support a depressive syndrome (possibly presenting with cognitive symptoms/pseudodementia) rather than a primary neurodegenerative process. This makes Alzheimer's disease (G30.9) less likely given the static rather than progressively worsening course, so it is removed. F03 remains as a differential given the degree of functional impairment in memory/organization, though F32.9 with cognitive dysfunction is now the leading diagnosis.", "is_final": false}

Just my same old routine, the same little blue and purple boxes every day.\n\nDoctor: Since the remodel began, how has your sleep been—are you sleeping through the night restfully, or do you notice snoring, gasping, or feeling unrefreshed when you wake up?\n\nPatient: Well, as for snoring or gasping, my husband hasn't mentioned a peep about anything like that, and he is a terribly light sleeper. If a car door slams three houses down the street, he's sitting bolt upright in bed! So I think my breathing is just fine in the night. And I *am* sleeping, I really am. I close my eyes and sleep right through till morning. \n\nBut restfully? Oh, doctor, that’s exactly the problem, and it's been driving me absolutely batty. I don't know why, but I wake up just so terribly tired even after sleeping a full eight or nine hours. It makes no sense! I’ll pull myself out of bed—and we have a lovely, heavy down comforter, so it's always a little hard to leave it anyway, especially when the mornings are crisp—and I’ll go into the bathroom to wash up. I just do my normal routine, you know, wash my face with a little warm water, put on my face cream, brush my teeth. Then I head downstairs to the kitchen to make breakfast. \n\nAnd it’s nothing complicated I'm making! Just putting the kettle on the stove for a cup of Earl Grey tea and popping a piece of wheat bread in the toaster. Maybe slicing half a grapefruit if they looked decent at the grocery store. But I tell you, by the time I wash up and make that simple little breakfast, I feel like I need to sit down again. I'm just completely wiped out. I'll get my plate to the kitchen table and just slump into my chair, and I haven't even really done anything yet! It’s so aggravating, feeling this completely drained just from getting the day started. I used to be up and at 'em, ready to tackle the laundry or the gardening, and now just buttering my toast feels like a chore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes like feeling unusually cold, dry skin, constipation, or weight gain over these past few months?\n\nPatient: Weight gain? Oh, goodness, no, it’s actually been quite the opposite! As for being cold or the dry skin, well, nothing out of the ordinary for a woman my age, I suppose. But the weight, now that is something I've definitely noticed, and it ties right back into what I was just saying about making breakfast. \n\nYou see, when I make that toast, I hardly even eat it. Most days I just sort of... pick at it. I'll pick at a piece of toast in the morning, or maybe have a few spoonfuls of soup for lunch, like a light tomato bisque or just some plain chicken broth. Food just simply doesn't sound good to me lately. It’s the strangest thing. I used to love cooking a nice, hearty pot roast on Sundays, with the tender carrots and the little red potatoes soaking up all the juices, but now... well, the thought of a big meal just makes me want to turn my head away. I look in the refrigerator and absolutely nothing looks appetizing. \n\nAnd because I'm just pecking like a little bird all day, my clothes are absolutely swimming on me! I went to put on my favorite pleated skirt last Sunday for church—the navy blue one I got at Macy's a few years ago, such lovely quality, you really don't find stitching like that anymore—and it has gotten so terribly loose! It practically slid right down my hips. I had to use a safety pin just to keep it up around my waist. I certainly haven't been trying to lose weight, heaven knows I don't need to be going on any diets at seventy-nine, you don't want to get too frail at this age. But without me even trying, the pounds are just dropping off and my skirts are hanging off me just because I can't bring myself to eat a proper meal.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like gardening, church activities, or spending time with friends, even if you don't feel sad?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s... it’s strange you say that, because that’s exactly it. I’m not sitting around crying into my handkerchief all day, I really aren't, but... well, the things I used to just love, they just sit there now. \n\nTake my tomato plants, for instance. I have always taken such pride in my tomatoes. Every spring, I’d get the soil just right, mix in a little peat moss and fertilizer—my husband used to help me with the heavy bags before his knees got so bad—and I’d plant these beautiful, sturdy little vines. I just loved the smell of the leaves when you brush up against them, you know that smell? But lately? The pots are just sitting there on the back patio. I look out the window and I know they need tending, I know the weeds are probably choking them out, but I just... I can't make myself care. \n\nAnd it’s the same with watching the birds. Right past the patio, we have this lovely cedar bird feeder my son-in-law built for me. I used to sit by the window every single morning, just waiting for the cardinals out back. There was this one bright red male, oh, he was just gorgeous, always so bossy with the other birds. I used to get such a thrill seeing him. Now, I see a flash of red out of the corner of my eye and I don't even bother to turn my head to look. \n\nEven my watercolors! I have a whole little setup in the corner of the sunroom. I took a class at the community center oh, it must be fifteen years ago now, and I just fell in love with painting little landscapes and flowers. I have all my good sable brushes laid out, my nice thick pressed paper, the little tubes of paint... and they're just gathering dust. I walk right past them. \n\nAnd the line dancing, too. My friends and I used to go down to the senior center every Thursday afternoon for line dancing. We’d do the Electric Slide, the Boot Scootin' Boogie, all of those fun ones. It was such a hoot, and such wonderful exercise for the joints. But when Shirley called me last week to see if I was coming, I just made up an excuse. I told her I was busy organizing the pantry. But I wasn't busy. I just couldn't muster up a single ounce of interest to go. It’s like all these wonderful things I used to love are just sitting there, waiting for me to pick them back up, and I just look at them and feel... nothing. I just can't make myself care about them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts that you're a burden to your family, feelings of guilt or worthlessness, or any thoughts that life isn't worth living?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no! No, no, no. I might be terribly tired, and my tomatoes might be dying on the vine, but I am certainly not ready to give up the ghost just yet! \n\nLife is absolutely worth living. I mean, I have seven grandchildren! The youngest, little Timmy, he just started middle school this fall, and he plays the trombone in the school band. Not very well yet, mind you, it sounds a bit like a dying goose sometimes when he practices, but I want to be around to hear him get better at it. I want to see him graduate. So no thoughts like that at all, goodness me. \n\nAnd as for being a burden or feeling worthless... well, I just don't see it that way. My husband, bless his heart, he tells me all the time he doesn't know what he'd do without me. Even if I'm just slumped in my armchair these days while he watches his golf tournaments on the television—you know how the announcers always whisper on those golf broadcasts? It used to drive me absolutely crazy, but now it's just sort of soothing background noise. Anyway, he just likes knowing I'm in the room with him. We've been married fifty-six years this coming August, and we take care of each other. I'm certainly not worthless to him. \n\nAnd my daughter Susan, she lives just two towns over, and she calls me every single Sunday after church without fail. She always says, 'Mom, you and Dad are the glue holding this family together.' So I certainly don't feel like a burden to them. If anything, they're always asking if they can come over and help *me* with things! Just last week Susan offered to come over and clean out the gutters, though I told her absolutely not, she has her own house and her own yard to worry about, and Arthur can hire the neighbor boy to do it if it really needs doing. \n\nSo no, doctor, I don't have any guilt or awful thoughts about not wanting to be here. I don't feel like a burden in the slightest. I just... I just don't have the pep I used to have, and I can't find the joy in my little hobbies right now. But I know my place in the world, and I know my family loves me. I just wish I had the energy to be my normal self for them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, let me just think for a second. Today is Thursday... yes, Thursday, October the 24th. I know for a fact it's Thursday because the garbage men always come early on Wednesday mornings, and just yesterday Arthur was complaining about how they banged the cans around out by the curb. He always gets so fussy about making sure the lids are on tight so the raccoons don't get in, you know how it is. 

And as for where we are, well, we're right here in your office at the clinic. The building right off of Elm Street. Although, I must say, we had the absolute hardest time finding a parking spot this morning! Arthur usually likes to park right up by the front doors because of his bad knees—I think I mentioned his knees earlier, didn't I, when we were talking about the tomatoes?—but there was some sort of big white maintenance truck taking up three whole spaces. Can you believe that? Three entire spaces for one truck! So we had to park all the way over by the pharmacy entrance and walk all the way through that long hallway with the linoleum floors. 

But yes, October 24th, and we are sitting right here in your examination room. I haven't lost my marbles just yet, doctor, I promise you that! My mind is as sharp as a tack, even if my body is just feeling terribly washed out these days.

Anyone would be worried when there are fans blowing day and night to dry out the floorboards, and you're just praying mold doesn't set in because my husband, God rest his soul, always used to say mold is the silent killer of a good foundation. But as for just sitting around feeling sad or... or unusually worried now that the worst of it is over? No, I wouldn't say that. \n\nI'm frustrated, certainly! Frustrated that I can't remember if I put the kettle on, or why I walked into the kitchen in the first place. Just yesterday, I went into the pantry for a tin of crushed tomatoes for my meatloaf—I always use the crushed ones, not the diced, because the diced just never break down right in the sauce—and I stood there staring at the cans for what felt like ten minutes, completely blank. But it's not sadness, dear. It's just... it's just this awful feeling of being disorganized in my own mind. I'm not crying into my tea or anything of that sort, and I'm not pacing the floors with worry, except for worrying about why my brain feels like it's stuffed with cotton wool. So, no, my mood itself is fine, it's just my memory that's driving me up the wall!\n\nDoctor: Over these past four months, would you say your memory and thinking have stayed about the same, or have they been gradually getting worse and worse?\n\nPatient: Well, that's a very good question, Doctor. I've been trying to put my finger on that very thing myself. I suppose... well, I wouldn't say it's a straight line down, if you know what I mean. It's not like every day is noticeably worse than the day before, like watching a tire slowly go flat. No, it's more like... it's just stayed consistently foggy since it started. \n\nI mean, I'm not entirely sure, to be perfectly honest. Some days I think, \"Oh, I'm doing a bit better today,\" like last Sunday when I managed to get through the entire church service and remember the pastor's main points for discussion at coffee hour afterward—we were talking about the book of Ruth, such a lovely story of loyalty, don't you think? But then the very next day, Monday, I'll go to the grocery store with a list of three things—just milk, bread, and those little peppermint candies I like to keep in my purse—and I'll come home with the milk and the peppermints, but completely forget the bread! And I'll have walked right past the bakery section, too. \n\nSo, I don't think it's necessarily getting worse and worse, like some sort of terrible decline. It just feels like I've hit this... this plateau of forgetfulness, and I'm just stuck here. It's been about the same level of frustrating for the whole four months. Just consistently thick and muddled, without really dipping lower, but certainly not clearing up either. It's just terribly stubborn, this fogginess.\n\nDoctor: Around the time your symptoms began during the home remodel, did you have any illnesses, infections, head injuries, or exposure to fumes, chemicals, or mold that you’re aware of?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that... illnesses or injuries. No, no head injuries, thank goodness. I've always been very careful on the stairs, especially after my friend Martha took a tumble last winter and fractured her hip—she was in rehab for weeks, poor thing, and the food there was just atrocious, she said. So I always hold the handrail. And I haven't been sick, not even a sniffle, which is a blessing because usually when the seasons change, I get a bit of a tickle in my throat.\n\nNow, as for the house... well, like I mentioned before, my husband always warned about mold. \"The silent killer,\" he called it. So when the pipe burst, that was my absolute biggest fear. I had them bring in those giant industrial fans—oh, the noise they made, it was like living on an airport runway! I had to turn the television up so loud just to hear Jeopardy. But the remediation company—such nice young men, very polite, always took their boots off at the door—they assured me they got everything dried out before any mold could start growing. They used these little moisture meter gadgets on the walls and everything. \n\nAnd chemicals or fumes? Well, there was the smell of the new paint, of course, when they finally got around to fixing the drywall. I chose a lovely shade called \"Alabaster\" for the hallway, it really brightens up the space compared to that dreary beige we had before. But it was just regular latex paint, nothing terribly strong, and I made sure to keep the windows cracked, even though it was a bit chilly, just to keep the air moving. I don't think I breathed in anything I shouldn't have. So, really, besides the dust from the drywall and the stress of having my entire downstairs turned upside down, I can't think of any sickness or strange fumes or anything like that. Just the ordinary chaos of a remodel, but nothing out of the ordinary, if you see what I mean.\n\nDoctor: Has this memory fogginess caused any real problems with managing your daily tasks—like paying bills, taking medications correctly, or handling your usual responsibilities—or have you still been able to keep up with everything despite the frustration?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the rub, isn't it? That's what really finally made me realize I couldn't just brush this off as being tired from the house repairs. Because, yes, it actually has started causing a mess of things, and I've always prided myself on being organized. I mean, I used to keep the whole household running like a Swiss watch! But lately... oh, goodness, it's just been one thing after another. \n\nFor instance, I completely missed my appointment with Dr. Henderson—my podiatrist, you know, he’s been helping me with this dreadful plantar fasciitis, which is a whole other story, let me tell you, the pain in the mornings! But I just completely forgot to go. Didn't even write it on the kitchen calendar. And then there’s the senior center. I volunteer there on Tuesdays and Thursdays, helping coordinate the activities schedule—bingo, the knitting circle, the guest speakers. Well, I got the dates so tangled up a few weeks ago that we had the watercolor instructor showing up at the exact same time as the gentleman who does the estate planning seminars! It was terribly embarrassing. We had to squeeze the painters into the small kitchenette area, and nobody was happy. \n\nIt’s gotten to the point where... well, it’s hard to admit, but my husband—bless his heart, he tries, even though he's usually the one asking me where his glasses are—he’s had to step in. He's been the one making the phone calls to the contractors to finish up the baseboards, and he's taken over managing the calendar because I just can't seem to keep it all straight anymore. I used to handle all of those decisions myself, easily! I’d juggle the bills, the appointments, the repairmen... and now I'm just looking at the paperwork and it feels like reading Greek. So, yes, it's definitely spilling over into my daily life, and I absolutely hate feeling like I have to be supervised in my own home.\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\": \"Screen for a reversible medication-induced cause (e.g., new sleep aids, anticholinergics, or other OTC/prescription drugs started during the stress\n\nPatient: Well, medications... let me think about that. You know, it's funny you ask, because my daughter—she's a nurse, actually, over at County General, works in the pediatric wing, such a demanding job but she loves the little ones—she's always asking me, \"Mom, what are you taking? Are you sure you're not mixing things up?\" And I tell her, I say, \"Susan, I am very careful with my pills.\" \n\nI have one of those little plastic organizers, you know the ones? With the days of the week printed on them in big bold letters. Mine has different colors for morning and evening—blue for the morning, because it reminds me of the sky, and a sort of dusky purple for the evening. It really helps keep things straight, especially now that things are so foggy. \n\nBut as for *new* medications... no, I really don't think so. I mean, I take my usual things. There's the little white pill for my blood pressure that Dr. Miller put me on years ago—oh, gosh, it must be five or six years now, ever since that time I got so worked up over the neighbor's dog digging up my prize-winning petunias. And then there's my calcium supplement, because at my age, you really have to watch your bone density, like I said about my friend Martha's hip. I take a multivitamin too, the kind for women over fifty, though frankly I've been over fifty for quite a while now! \n\nDuring the worst of the remodel stress, when the noise was just unbearable and the dust was everywhere, I did have a few nights where I just couldn't drift off. I'd lie there staring at the ceiling, worrying about whether the insurance was going to cover the new flooring in the hallway. I might have taken a Tylenol PM once or twice, just to help me settle down. But that was months ago, and just a couple of times. I certainly haven't started taking anything new on a regular basis, no new prescriptions or anything like that. Just my same old routine, the same little blue and purple boxes every day.\n\nDoctor: Since the remodel began, how has your sleep been—are you sleeping through the night restfully, or do you notice snoring, gasping, or feeling unrefreshed when you wake up?\n\nPatient: Well, as for snoring or gasping, my husband hasn't mentioned a peep about anything like that, and he is a terribly light sleeper. If a car door slams three houses down the street, he's sitting bolt upright in bed! So I think my breathing is just fine in the night. And I *am* sleeping, I really am. I close my eyes and sleep right through till morning. \n\nBut restfully? Oh, doctor, that’s exactly the problem, and it's been driving me absolutely batty. I don't know why, but I wake up just so terribly tired even after sleeping a full eight or nine hours. It makes no sense! Just putting the kettle on the stove for a cup of Earl Grey tea and popping a piece of wheat bread in the toaster. Maybe slicing half a grapefruit if they looked decent at the grocery store. I'll get my plate to the kitchen table and just slump into my chair, and I haven't even really done anything yet! But the weight, now that is something I've definitely noticed, and it ties right back into what I was just saying about making breakfast. \n\nYou see, when I make that toast, I hardly even eat it. Most days I just sort of... pick at it. I'll pick at a piece of toast in the morning, or maybe have a few spoonfuls of soup for lunch, like a light tomato bisque or just some plain chicken broth. Food just simply doesn't sound good to me lately. It’s the strangest thing. I used to love cooking a nice, hearty pot roast on Sundays, with the tender carrots and the little red potatoes soaking up all the juices, but now... well, the thought of a big meal just makes me want to turn my head away. But without me even trying, the pounds are just dropping off and my skirts are hanging off me just because I can't bring myself to eat a proper meal.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like gardening, church activities, or spending time with friends, even if you don't feel sad?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s... it’s strange you say that, because that’s exactly it. My friends and I used to go down to the senior center every Thursday afternoon for line dancing. We’d do the Electric Slide, the Boot Scootin' Boogie, all of those fun ones. But I wasn't busy. I just couldn't muster up a single ounce of interest to go. It’s like all these wonderful things I used to love are just sitting there, waiting for me to pick them back up, and I just look at them and feel... nothing. I just can't make myself care about them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts that you're a burden to your family, feelings of guilt or worthlessness, or any thoughts that life isn't worth living?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no! Not very well yet, mind you, it sounds a bit like a dying goose sometimes when he practices, but I want to be around to hear him get better at it. I want to see him graduate. Just last week Susan offered to come over and clean out the gutters, though I told her absolutely not, she has her own house and her own yard to worry about, and Arthur can hire the neighbor boy to do it if it really needs doing. \n\nSo no, doctor, I don't have any guilt or awful thoughts about not wanting to be here. I don't feel like a burden in the slightest. I just... I just don't have the pep I used to have, and I can't find the joy in my little hobbies right now. But I know my place in the world, and I know my family loves me. I just wish I had the energy to be my normal self for them.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me today's date and where we are right now?\n\nPatient: Well, let me just think for a second. Today is Thursday... yes, Thursday, October the 24th. I know for a fact it's Thursday because the garbage men always come early on Wednesday mornings, and just yesterday Arthur was complaining about how they banged the cans around out by the curb. He always gets so fussy about making sure the lids are on tight so the raccoons don't get in, you know how it is. \n\nAnd as for where we are, well, we're right here in your office at the clinic. The building right off of Elm Street. Although, I must say, we had the absolute hardest time finding a parking spot this morning! Arthur usually likes to park right up by the front doors because of his bad knees—I think I mentioned his knees earlier, didn't I, when we were talking about the tomatoes?—but there was some sort of big white maintenance truck taking up three whole spaces. Can you believe that? Three entire spaces for one truck! So we had to park all the way over by the pharmacy entrance and walk all the way through that long hallway with the linoleum floors. \n\nBut yes, October 24th, and we are sitting right here in your examination room. I haven't lost my marbles just yet, doctor, I promise you that! My mind is as sharp as a tack, even if my body is just feeling terribly washed out these days."
